    Part 8: CGOA Exam Preparation ▸ Chapter 2 of 2 ▸ Next: Appendices (Command Reference, Glossary, Troubleshooting, and more)

    30.1 Lab 1: Install ArgoCD and Deploy an Application
          - Install ArgoCD on a Kubernetes cluster
          - Create an Application pointing to your Git repo
          - Configure auto-sync
          - Verify deployment and health
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ $ kubectl create namespace argocd                                                │
          │ $ kubectl apply -n argocd -f install.yaml   # from Ch5.2's install URL           │
          │ $ kubectl -n argocd get pods -w                                                  │
          │ # once all argocd-* pods are Running:                                            │
          │ $ argocd app create easypj-web \                                                 │
          │ $   --repo https://github.com/yourorg/easypj-config.git \                        │
          │ $   --path overlays/dev --dest-server https://kubernetes.default.svc \           │
          │ $   --dest-namespace easypj --sync-policy automated                              │
          │ $ argocd app get easypj-web                                                      │
          │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
          │ EXPECTED OUTPUT:                                                                 │
          │ NAME        CLUSTER                          NAMESPACE  STATUS  HEALTH           │
          │ easypj-web  https://kubernetes.default.svc   easypj     Synced  Healthy          │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ 3 pods Running, Service exposing port 8000, health checks passing.               │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.2 Lab 2: Configure Secrets with Sealed Secrets
          - Install Sealed Secrets controller
          - Create a Kubernetes Secret
          - Seal it with kubeseal
          - Commit SealedSecret to Git
          - Verify decryption and pod access
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ $ kubectl apply -f sealed-secrets-controller.yaml   # from Ch13's install URL    │
          │ $ kubectl create secret generic easypj-db-creds -n easypj \                      │
          │ $   --from-literal=DATABASE_URL=postgresql://easypj:pw@postgres:5432/easypj \    │
          │ $   --dry-run=client -o yaml > secret.yaml                                       │
          │ $ kubeseal --format yaml < secret.yaml > sealed-secret.yaml                      │
          │ $ rm secret.yaml   # never commit the plaintext version                          │
          │ $ git add sealed-secret.yaml && git commit -m 'Add sealed DB creds'              │
          │ $ git push                                                                       │
          │ $ kubectl get sealedsecret,secret easypj-db-creds -n easypj                      │
          │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
          │ EXPECTED OUTPUT:                                                                 │
          │ NAME                                       SYNCED   AGE                          │
          │ sealedsecret.bitnami.com/easypj-db-creds   True     45s                          │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ NAME                     TYPE     DATA   AGE                                     │
          │ secret/easypj-db-creds   Opaque   1      44s                                     │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ The SealedSecret decrypted into a real Secret automatically — the                │
          │ controller did it, you never ran kubectl create secret against the cluster.      │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.3 Lab 3: Multi-Environment Deployment with Kustomize
          - Create base/ and overlays/ directory
          - Configure dev, staging, prod overlays
          - Deploy via ArgoCD ApplicationSet
          - Verify each environment is correct
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ $ mkdir -p base overlays/{dev,staging,prod}                                      │
          │ # base/ holds deployment.yaml, service.yaml, configmap.yaml + kustomization      │
          │ # each overlays/<env>/kustomization.yaml patches replicas + image tag            │
          │ $ kubectl kustomize overlays/prod | head -5                                      │
          │ $ git add . && git commit -m 'Add 3-env Kustomize overlays' && git push          │
          │ $ argocd appset create environments-appset.yaml                                  │
          │ $ argocd app list                                                                │
          │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
          │ EXPECTED OUTPUT:                                                                 │
          │ NAME               NAMESPACE  STATUS  HEALTH   REPLICAS                          │
          │ easypj-dev          easypj-dev     Synced  Healthy  1                            │
          │ easypj-staging       easypj-staging Synced  Healthy  2                           │
          │ easypj-prod          easypj-prod    Synced  Healthy  5                           │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ One ApplicationSet, one Git push, 3 environments deployed with                   │
          │ environment-appropriate replica counts — zero manual per-env commands.           │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.4 Lab 4: Drift Detection and Auto-Healing
          - Deploy application with auto-sync and self-heal
          - Manually change replicas (kubectl scale)
          - Observe ArgoCD detecting drift
          - Observe auto-correction
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ $ kubectl get deployment easypj-web -n easypj -o jsonpath='{.spec.replicas}'     │
          │ $ kubectl scale deployment easypj-web -n easypj --replicas=5                     │
          │ $ argocd app get easypj-web   # run again ~30-60s later                          │
          │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
          │ EXPECTED OUTPUT:                                                                 │
          │ # immediately after scaling:                                                     │
          │ Health Status:  Healthy                                                          │
          │ Sync Status:    OutOfSync                                                        │
          │   Deployment  easypj/easypj-web  OutOfSync  replicas: live=5, desired=3          │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ # ~1 poll interval later (selfHeal: true):                                       │
          │ Sync Status:    Synced                                                           │
          │   replicas back to 3 — ArgoCD reverted your manual kubectl scale.                │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ You just watched State Drift get detected AND reconciled (Ch31.3),               │
          │ not just read about it.                                                          │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.5 Lab 5: Rollback with GitOps
          - Deploy v1 of easypj
          - Deploy v2 (with intentional issue)
          - Rollback via git revert
          - Observe ArgoCD syncing back to v1
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ # assume v2's config repo commit introduced a bad readiness probe                │
          │ $ git log --oneline -3                                                           │
          │ #   a1b2c3d Bump easypj to v2 (BAD — readiness probe misconfigured)              │
          │ #   9f8e7d6 Bump easypj to v1                                                    │
          │ $ git revert a1b2c3d                                                             │
          │ $ git push                                                                       │
          │ $ argocd app get easypj-web -w                                                   │
          │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
          │ EXPECTED OUTPUT:                                                                 │
          │ Revert commit created — Git now shows the v1 image tag again.                    │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ Sync Status:  OutOfSync -> Syncing -> Synced                                     │
          │ Health Status: Progressing -> Healthy                                            │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ Pods roll back to the v1 image. Total time from git revert to a                  │
          │ healthy rollback: ~2-3 minutes, no SSH, no manual kubectl anywhere.              │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.6 Lab 6: FluxCD Bootstrap and Deployment
          - Bootstrap FluxCD on a cluster
          - Create GitRepository and Kustomization
          - Deploy easypj via Flux
          - Verify reconciliation
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ $ flux bootstrap github --owner=yourorg --repository=easypj-config \             │
          │ $   --path=clusters/dev --personal                                               │
          │ $ flux create source git easypj \                                                │
          │ $   --url=https://github.com/yourorg/easypj-config --branch=main --interval=1m   │
          │ $ flux create kustomization easypj \                                             │
          │ $   --source=easypj --path='./overlays/dev' --prune=true --interval=5m           │
          │ $ flux get kustomizations                                                        │
          │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
          │ EXPECTED OUTPUT:                                                                 │
          │ NAME     REVISION        SUSPENDED  READY  MESSAGE                               │
          │ easypj   main@sha1:7c2a  False      True   Applied revision: main@sha1:7c2a      │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ flux-system namespace now running all 5 controllers, reconciling                 │
          │ easypj-config on a 5-minute Kustomization interval.                              │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.7 Lab 7: ArgoCD Notifications Setup
          - Configure notification triggers
          - Set up Slack webhook
          - Test notification on sync success/failure
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ $ kubectl apply -n argocd -f notifications-configmap.yaml                        │
          │ $ kubectl apply -n argocd -f notifications-secret.yaml   # Slack webhook URL     │
          │ $ argocd app get easypj-web -o json | jq '.metadata.annotations'                 │
          │ # trigger a real event to test the wiring:                                       │
          │ $ kubectl scale deployment easypj-web -n easypj --replicas=1                     │
          │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
          │ EXPECTED OUTPUT:                                                                 │
          │ notifications.argoproj.io/subscribe.on-sync-status-unknown.slack:                │
          │   gitops-alerts                                                                  │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ #gitops-alerts (Slack)                                                           │
          │ 🔴 easypj-web went OutOfSync (replicas: live=1, desired=3)                        │
          │ 🟢 easypj-web is back Synced and Healthy                                          │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ Two messages, no polling the ArgoCD UI to notice — the Feedback                  │
          │ Loop (Ch31.6) surfaced both transitions automatically.                           │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.8 Lab 8: ApplicationSet for Environments
          - Create cluster generator ApplicationSet
          - Deploy to multiple namespaces
          - Verify all applications are created
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ $ kubectl apply -f environments-appset.yaml                                      │
          │ $ argocd appset get environments                                                 │
          │ $ argocd app list --output name                                                  │

          │ EXPECTED OUTPUT:                                                                 │
          │ NAME          NAMESPACE       CLUSTER    STATUS                                  │
          │ easypj-dev     easypj-dev      in-cluster Synced                                 │
          │ easypj-staging easypj-staging  in-cluster Synced                                 │
          │ easypj-prod    easypj-prod     in-cluster Synced                                 │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ 3 Applications generated from ONE ApplicationSet + ONE cluster                   │
          │ generator — no per-namespace Application YAML was hand-written.                  │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.9 Lab 9: Progressive Delivery with Argo Rollouts
          - Install Argo Rollouts
          - Create Rollout resource for easypj
          - Perform canary deployment
          - Observe traffic shifting
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ $ kubectl argo rollouts get rollout easypj-web -n easypj --watch                 │
          │ # in a second terminal:                                                          │
          │ $ kubectl argo rollouts set image easypj-web easypj=easypj:v2 -n easypj          │
          │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
          │ EXPECTED OUTPUT:                                                                 │
          │ Name:            easypj-web                                                      │
          │ Strategy:        Canary                                                          │
          │   Step:          1/5                                                             │
          │   SetWeight:     20                                                              │
          │   ActualWeight:  20                                                              │
          │ Images:          easypj:v1 (stable)  easypj:v2 (canary, 20% traffic)             │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ # after metric analysis passes each step:                                        │
          │   Step:  5/5    SetWeight: 100   Images: easypj:v2 (stable)                      │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ Traffic shifted 20% -> 40% -> 60% -> 80% -> 100%, pausing between                │
          │ steps for the configured AnalysisTemplate to pass (Ch21).                        │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    30.10 Lab 10: Full GitOps CI/CD Pipeline
           - Create GitHub Actions workflow
           - Build and push Docker image
           - Update config repo with new tag
           - Verify ArgoCD auto-syncs the update
          ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
          │ # .github/workflows/deploy.yml runs on push to main:                             │
          │ #  1. docker build -t registry/easypj:$(git rev-parse --short HEAD) .            │
          │ #  2. docker push registry/easypj:$(git rev-parse --short HEAD)                  │
          │ #  3. yq -i '.spec.template.spec.containers[0].image = "...:SHA"' \              │
          │ #       easypj-config/overlays/dev/deployment-patch.yaml                         │
          │ #  4. git commit + push to easypj-config                                         │
          │ $ git push origin main   # triggers the whole chain above                        │
          │ $ argocd app get easypj-web -w                                                   │
          │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
          │ EXPECTED OUTPUT:                                                                 │
          │ GitHub Actions: build ✅ -> push ✅ -> commit config repo ✅                        │
          │                                                                                  │
          │ ArgoCD detects the new commit within one poll (or instantly via                  │
          │ webhook, Ch11) -> Syncing -> Synced -> Healthy, running the exact                │
          │ image the CI job just built. Zero manual steps between git push                  │
          │ and the new version running in the cluster.                                      │
          └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD — Why These 10 Labs, Specifically, and Not Others
    ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    The 10 labs aren't a random tour of features — they're chosen to each
    isolate ONE testable CGOA behavior you can only truly verify by watching
    it happen: Lab 4 proves drift correction is automatic (not just
    described in a diagram), Lab 5 proves git revert really does trigger a
    sync, Lab 9 proves a canary really does shift traffic in steps. Reading
    about self-healing and watching your own kubectl scale get reverted in
    real time are different levels of understanding — the second one is what
    survives under exam pressure when a scenario question asks you to
    predict what happens next, because you've already seen it happen once.

    ⚠️ GOTCHAS
    ──────────
    • Reading through the labs instead of running them — the entire
      pedagogical point is watching predicted behavior actually occur;
      reading is Chapter 1-29, this chapter is deliberately different.
    • Skipping labs that cover material you feel confident about (e.g.
      skipping Lab 1 because Chapter 5 felt easy) — the labs test
      RETENTION under hands-on conditions, a different skill than
      recognizing a concept in a diagram.
    • Not timing yourself on Lab 4 or Lab 5 — these two are the closest
      simulation of a live troubleshooting scenario question; practicing
      them under a rough time limit builds the same muscle the exam's
      90-second budget requires.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
    ─────────────
    • If you can only redo 2 labs before exam day, make them Lab 4
      (drift/self-heal) and Lab 5 (rollback) — these two map to the
      most-repeated exam concepts across the entire guide.
    • Each lab secretly maps to a chapter: Lab 1→Ch5, Lab 2→Ch13, Lab
      3→Ch3+10, Lab 4→Ch4, Lab 5→Ch2+8, Lab 6→Ch16, Lab 7→Ch11, Lab
      8→Ch10, Lab 9→Ch21, Lab 10→Ch25.
    • A scenario question that describes a multi-step troubleshooting or
      verification process is testing whether you've actually DONE
      something like Lab 4 or Lab 10 — not whether you've read about it.

    ────────────────────

    commands appear in the next hands-on chapter.

Chapter 31: GitOps Terminology — The Official CGOA Vocabulary
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🎯 Exam-Critical · ⏱️ ~1.5 hours · 🎯 CGOA: GitOps Terminology (20%) · 🏢 Daily-Work: Precise vocabulary for incident reports and design docs

    WHY THIS CHAPTER MATTERS
    ────────────────────────
    This chapter exists because of a hard truth about the CGOA exam: you can
    understand every mechanism in Chapters 1-30 perfectly and still lose
    points if you don't know the EXACT official term for a concept you
    already grasp intuitively. Terminology is 20% of the exam — its own
    domain, separate from Principles — and it rewards precision of language,
    not just understanding.

    🎯 LEARNING OBJECTIVES — by the end of this chapter you can:
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Define all 9 official CGOA terminology items in the Linux
         Foundation's own phrasing
      2. Distinguish Declarative Description (the artifact) from Desired
         State (what it means)
      3. Distinguish State Drift (the difference) from State
         Reconciliation (the fix)
      4. State the boundary of a GitOps Managed Software System — what's
         in scope and what isn't
      5. Explain the Feedback Loop as the mechanism connecting Chapters
         11, 19, and 26

    📍 YOU ARE HERE
    ────────────────
    Part 9: The Complete CGOA Curriculum ▸ Chapter 1 of 3 ▸ Next: Chapter 32 (Related Practices)

    31.1  Continuous
    The word "continuous" is attached to several different processes in
    software delivery, and CGOA expects you to tell them apart precisely,
    not just recognize the word:

         [THE 4 MEANINGS OF "CONTINUOUS" — precisely distinguished]
         ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 1. Continuous Integration (CI)                                             │
         │    Merging code changes frequently (often multiple times a day), each      │
         │    verified automatically by a build + test pipeline.                      │
         │ ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ 2. Continuous Delivery                                                     │
         │    CI, plus every change that passes is automatically packaged into a      │
         │    release that COULD be deployed at any time — a human still decides      │
         │    WHEN to actually deploy it.                                             │
         │ ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ 3. Continuous Deployment                                                   │
         │    Continuous Delivery, plus every change that passes is automatically     │
         │    deployed to production with no human approval gate at all.              │
         │ ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ 4. Continuous Reconciliation — the GitOps-specific one                     │
         │    The reconciliation loop (Ch4.4) never stops running. It isn't a         │
         │    one-time "deploy event" — it observes, diffs, and acts forever,         │
         │    for as long as the GitOps Managed Software System exists (31.4).        │
         └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    GitOps REQUIRES Continuous Reconciliation by definition (Ch4's 4th
    principle). It does NOT require Continuous Deployment — a team at GitOps
    Maturity Level 2 (Ch4.5) with manual sync is still doing GitOps; they've
    simply chosen a human approval gate before each reconciliation cycle
    acts. Don't assume "GitOps" implies "fully automated deploys with zero
    human oversight."

    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ 📖 KEY TERM: Continuous (CGOA)                                          │
    │ A process that runs repeatedly and indefinitely rather than once. In   │
    │ GitOps specifically, this describes the reconciliation loop, which     │
    │ never "finishes" — it keeps comparing desired vs. actual state for the │
    │ lifetime of the managed system.                                        │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    31.2  Declarative Description & Desired State
    Chapters 1 and 3 already taught you to write and read declarative YAML —
    this section gives the two related ideas their official, separate names,
    because CGOA tests them as distinct terms:

         [DECLARATIVE DESCRIPTION vs DESIRED STATE — the exam distinction]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ DECLARATIVE DESCRIPTION                 DESIRED STATE                        │
         │ The written artifact itself —           What that artifact MEANS once        │
         │ the YAML/JSON file(s) you commit        a reconciler interprets it —         │
         │ to the state store.                     the abstract target: "3 pods         │
         │                                          of easypj-web, port 8000".          │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ Example: deployment.yaml with           Example: "exactly 3 healthy          │
         │ "replicas: 3" written in it.            replicas should exist".              │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ 💡 KEY INSIGHT: Different Declarative Descriptions can encode the SAME        │
         │    Desired State. Raw YAML, a Kustomize overlay, and a rendered Helm         │
         │    chart can all describe "3 replicas of easypj-web" — three different       │
         │    descriptions, one desired state. The reconciler cares about the           │
         │    STATE, not which description technique produced it.                       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    31.3  State Drift & State Reconciliation
    Chapter 4.4 taught you drift and reconciliation as a story ("Alice
    manually scales a pod"). Here are the two formal, separately-testable
    terms:

         • State Drift — the MEASURED DIFFERENCE between desired state
           (from the Declarative Description) and actual state
           (observed from the live system), at a specific point in time.
         • State Reconciliation — the ACT of resolving that difference,
           bringing actual state back in line with desired state.

    These are NOT the same event. Drift can be DETECTED without being
    RECONCILED — a manual-sync-only Application (Ch7.4) will happily show
    "OutOfSync" (drift detected) indefinitely until a human runs argocd app
    sync (reconciliation, triggered manually rather than automatically).
    Reconciliation specifically implies the CORRECTIVE ACTION, not just
    noticing the problem.

    31.4  The GitOps Managed Software System
    This official term names something every chapter so far has assumed
    without naming: the boundary of what GitOps actually controls.

    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ 📖 KEY TERM: GitOps Managed Software System                                   │
    │ The complete set of resources — applications, configuration, and             │
    │ optionally infrastructure — whose desired state is defined in a              │
    │ Declarative Description, stored in a State Store, and continuously           │
    │ reconciled by a GitOps agent.                                                │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    Not everything touching your stack is automatically part of it. Ask one
    test question: "is there a Declarative Description AND active
    reconciliation for this specific thing?"

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ IN the GitOps Managed Software System:                                       │
         │   ✅ easypj-web's Deployment (Ch1) — described + reconciled by ArgoCD         │
         │   ✅ A Sealed Secret (Ch13) — described + reconciled, decrypted in place      │
         │   ✅ An RDS instance via Crossplane (Ch23) — described + reconciled           │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ NOT in the GitOps Managed Software System (even if related):                 │
         │   ❌ A database's actual DATA/rows — no reconciler manages row content,       │
         │      only schema/migrations might be (via a PreSync hook Job, Ch11.5)        │
         │   ❌ A cloud IAM change made by hand in a provider console, bypassing         │
         │      Crossplane entirely — no Declarative Description exists for it          │
         │   ❌ A legacy VM nothing in Git ever describes — outside the boundary         │
         │      even if it sits in the same network as everything else                  │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    31.5  State Store
    Every diagram in this guide up to now has used "Git repo" and "config
    repo" interchangeably with the general concept — here is the formal,
    tool-agnostic term the exam uses:

    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ 📖 KEY TERM: State Store                                                │
    │ The durable, versioned location where the Declarative Description is   │
    │ stored and from which a reconciler reads. Git is the default and most  │
    │ common State Store in GitOps — but not the only valid one.             │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    What makes something suitable as a State Store: durability (it survives
    a restart), versioning/history (you can see and revert prior states —
    Rollback, 31.7, depends on this), and ideally an audit trail of who
    changed what. Chapter 33.3 covers real, production alternatives to Git —
    OCI registries and object storage buckets — both already used by
    FluxCD's own source controller (Ch16.2) without this guide ever naming
    them as "alternative state stores" until now.

    31.6  The Feedback Loop
    You've already built three-quarters of this concept without its name:
    ArgoCD notifications (Ch11), Flux Alerts (Ch19), and GitOps
    observability metrics (Ch26) are all implementations of ONE official
    idea.

    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ 📖 KEY TERM: Feedback Loop                                              │
    │ The mechanism by which reconciliation STATUS (synced/healthy, drifted, │
    │ failed) is surfaced back to humans and other systems — closing the gap │
    │ between "the system attempted to reconcile" and "a human knows whether │
    │ it worked."                                                            │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    Without a Feedback Loop, GitOps degrades into fire-and-forget: the
    reconciler could be failing silently for hours, and the first signal
    anyone gets is a user-reported outage. Every notification trigger, every
    Grafana panel, every Slack alert in this guide is a Feedback Loop
    component — this section just gives the umbrella concept its official
    name.

    31.7  Rollback
    The formal definition, tying together the two mechanisms Chapters 2 and
    8 already taught:

    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ 📖 KEY TERM: Rollback                                                   │
    │ Reverting a GitOps Managed Software System to a previous, known-good   │
    │ Desired State.                                                         │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         | Mechanism              | Durability                  | Speed     |
         | git revert             | Permanent — recorded in the | ~2-3 min  |
         |                        | State Store's history       |           |
         | argocd app rollback /  | Temporary — live cluster    | Instant   |
         | flux equivalent        | only; Git still shows the   |           |
         |                        | newer (bad) commit          |           |

    CGOA expects you to know Rollback is fundamentally a State Store (Git)
    history operation in the canonical GitOps model — tool-native rollback
    is a faster but less durable variant, not a replacement for it (Ch8.5's
    Under the Hood covers exactly why).

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD — How OpenGitOps Formalized These Terms
    ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    Every term in this chapter traces back to the CNCF OpenGitOps working
    group's formal principles document (opengitops.dev), which the Linux
    Foundation's CGOA exam is built directly on top of. That's not a
    coincidence you need to research further — it's the reason this chapter
    exists: OpenGitOps deliberately separated "principles" (the 4 rules —
    Ch1-30 already cover these in depth) from "terminology" (the vocabulary
    used to TALK about systems following those rules — this chapter). A tool
    can satisfy all 4 principles while the engineer operating it still can't
    name what just happened in a State Store or a Feedback Loop — that gap
    is exactly what Terminology (20% of the exam) tests.

    ⚠️ GOTCHAS
    ──────────
    • Confusing "drift detected" with "drift reconciled" — a manual-sync
      Application shows drift status (State Drift, 31.3) for as long as a
      human delays running sync; the drift isn't reconciled until the sync
      actually runs.
    • Assuming every resource touching your cluster is part of the GitOps
      Managed Software System (31.4) just because SOME of your stack is —
      always apply the "Declarative Description AND active reconciliation"
      test to the SPECIFIC resource in question, not the stack as a whole.
    • Treating "Continuous Deployment" and "Continuous Reconciliation"
      (31.1) as synonyms — GitOps mandates the latter always; it mandates
      the former only if you've chosen full auto-sync (Maturity Level 3,
      Ch4.5) rather than a manual approval gate.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
    ─────────────
    • If an exam question uses the EXACT phrase "GitOps Managed Software
      System," it is testing the scope/boundary concept from 31.4 — look
      for the option that correctly excludes something outside the
      Declarative-Description-plus-reconciliation boundary.
    • When a question mentions Git AND asks about
      durability/versioning/audit trail together, it's testing the general
      "State Store" concept (31.5), not Git specifically — the correct
      answer usually generalizes beyond Git.
    • "Feedback Loop" questions (31.6) are really asking you to recognize
      notifications/alerts/observability as ONE concept wearing three
      different chapter numbers (Ch11, Ch19, Ch26) — don't overthink it as
      something new.

    🔑 COMMAND REFERENCE
    ────────────────────
    None yet — this chapter is conceptual. Your first real
    commands appear in the next hands-on chapter.

    🏋️ PRACTICE DRILL — Term-Matching Under Exam Conditions
    ─────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    The CGOA exam's Terminology domain frequently presents a short scenario
    and asks you to identify which OFFICIAL term it describes — not to
    explain the mechanism, which you already know from Ch1-30.
    TASK:
       Match each scenario below to exactly one term from this chapter
       (Continuous, Declarative Description, Desired State, State Drift,
       State Reconciliation, GitOps Managed Software System, State Store,
       Feedback Loop, Rollback). Scenario 1: an engineer runs kubectl
       scale --replicas=5 directly against the cluster, and ArgoCD's next
       Diff cycle flags a mismatch against Git's replicas: 3. Scenario 2:
       a Slack channel receives a message the moment an Application's
       health transitions from Healthy to Degraded. Scenario 3: a team
       stores its Kustomize overlays in an OCI-compliant container
       registry instead of a Git repository. Scenario 4: git revert
       a1b2c3d && git push is used to undo a bad production config change
       from two hours ago. Scenario 5: the abstract target of "3 healthy
       replicas of easypj-web," as opposed to the YAML file that expresses
       it. (Answers: 1-State Drift, 2-Feedback Loop, 3-State Store,
       4-Rollback, 5-Desired State.)

    📊 VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ ▸ Continuous                                                         │
    │   4 meanings: CI, Continuous Delivery, Continuous Deployment,        │
    │   Continuous Reconciliation — GitOps mandates only the last.         │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ Description vs State                                               │
    │   Declarative Description = the file. Desired State = what the       │
    │   file means. Many descriptions, one state.                          │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ Drift vs Reconciliation                                            │
    │   Drift = the measured difference. Reconciliation = the              │
    │   corrective action. Detecting is not fixing.                        │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ Managed System Boundary                                            │
    │   In scope only if BOTH a Declarative Description exists AND         │
    │   active reconciliation applies to that specific resource.           │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ State Store                                                        │
    │   The durable, versioned home of the Declarative Description.        │
    │   Git is the default, not the only option (see Ch33.3).              │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ Feedback Loop                                                      │
    │   The status-surfacing mechanism — Ch11 notifications + Ch19         │
    │   alerts + Ch26 observability are all one concept.                   │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    Chapter 31 — CGOA Exam Focus:
         🎯 All 9 official Terminology domain terms, precisely defined
         🎯 The Declarative Description vs. Desired State distinction
         🎯 The State Drift vs. State Reconciliation distinction
         🎯 GitOps Managed Software System scope/boundary reasoning


Chapter 32: Related Practices — CaC, IaC, DevOps & DevSecOps, CI/CD
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🎯 Exam-Critical · ⏱️ ~1.5 hours · 🎯 CGOA: Related Practices (16%) · 🏢 Daily-Work: Positioning GitOps correctly among neighboring practices

    WHY THIS CHAPTER MATTERS
    ────────────────────────
    GitOps doesn't exist in isolation — it's one practice among several that
    overlap in tooling and vocabulary but answer different questions. The
    Related Practices domain tests whether you can correctly place GitOps
    relative to Configuration as Code, Infrastructure as Code, DevOps,
    DevSecOps, CI, and CD — a skill separate from knowing how GitOps itself
    works, and one this guide has only taught by example until now.

    🎯 LEARNING OBJECTIVES — by the end of this chapter you can:
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Distinguish Configuration as Code (CaC) from Infrastructure as
         Code (IaC) by what each provisions vs. configures
      2. Explain GitOps as a specific, stricter implementation of CaC —
         not a synonym for it
      3. Map GitOps onto the DevOps "Three Ways" (Flow, Feedback,
         Continuous Learning)
      4. Define DevSecOps and identify GitOps's 3 concrete DevSecOps
         mechanisms already covered in Ch2, Ch24, and Ch27
      5. State the precise boundary between CI and CD, and where GitOps
         sits in that boundary

    📍 YOU ARE HERE
    ────────────────
    Part 9: The Complete CGOA Curriculum ▸ Chapter 2 of 3 ▸ Next: Chapter 33 (Architecture & Tooling Patterns)

    32.1  Configuration as Code (CaC)
    Configuration as Code is the umbrella practice this entire guide has
    been demonstrating since Chapter 1, without naming it: representing the
    configuration of a system — not the system's underlying infrastructure,
    but how it's set up and run — as versioned text files instead of manual,
    undocumented changes.

    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ 📖 KEY TERM: Configuration as Code (CaC)                                │
    │ The practice of storing a system's configuration in version-controlled │
    │ text files (YAML, JSON, HCL, etc.) rather than applying it through     │
    │ manual, undocumented steps — enabling review, history, and repeatable  │
    │ application of that configuration.                                     │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    GitOps is best understood as CaC PLUS three extra, stricter
    requirements: the config must live in Git specifically (a State Store,
    per Ch31.5), an agent must PULL it automatically, and that agent must
    reconcile CONTINUOUSLY. Plain CaC (e.g., a config file you `scp` to a
    server, or an Ansible playbook you run by hand from your laptop)
    satisfies none of the last two — it's versioned and declarative, but not
    automatically pulled or continuously enforced. Every GitOps setup is
    CaC; not every CaC setup is GitOps.

         [CaC vs GitOps — GitOps is CaC with 2 extra hard requirements]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ CaC (general)                          GitOps (specific, stricter)           │
         │ Config stored as versioned text        Config stored as versioned text       │
         │ Applied by any means — scp, Ansible    Applied ONLY by an agent that         │
         │   run by hand, manual `kubectl apply`    pulls automatically & reconciles    │
         │ No requirement to keep re-checking     Continuously re-checked against       │
         │   after the initial apply                the live system, forever            │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    32.2  Infrastructure as Code (IaC)
    IaC answers a different question than CaC: not "how is this application
    configured?" but "does the underlying infrastructure (VMs, networks,
    managed databases, the Kubernetes cluster itself) exist at all, and in
    what shape?" Chapter 23 already showed you IaC in practice via
    Crossplane — this section gives it its formal name and draws the
    boundary against CaC precisely.

    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ 📖 KEY TERM: Infrastructure as Code (IaC)                               │
    │ The practice of provisioning and managing infrastructure — compute,    │
    │ networking, managed services — through versioned declarative           │
    │ definitions, rather than manual console clicks or ad-hoc scripts.      │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

         [IaC provisions the platform; CaC/GitOps configures what runs on it]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ LAYER                    PRACTICE   EXAMPLE FROM THIS GUIDE                  │
         │ The Kubernetes cluster    IaC        Ch23: Crossplane provisions an EKS      │
         │   itself, an RDS                       cluster / RDS instance                │
         │   instance, a VPC                                                            │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ What runs ON the         CaC /      Ch1-22: the easypj-web Deployment,       │
         │   cluster once it          GitOps     Service, ConfigMap — ArgoCD/Flux       │
         │   exists                               reconciling them                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    The line blurs in one specific, exam-relevant case: Crossplane (Ch23)
    lets a GitOps agent manage IaC resources too, by representing
    infrastructure (an RDS instance) as a Kubernetes Custom Resource. That
    doesn't erase the conceptual distinction — it means Crossplane brings
    IaC INTO the GitOps Managed Software System (Ch31.4) rather than the two
    practices becoming the same thing. You can have IaC without GitOps (raw
    Terraform run from a laptop) and GitOps without IaC (ArgoCD managing
    only application manifests on a pre-existing cluster) — they're
    independent axes.

    32.3  DevOps and the Three Ways
    DevOps is often reduced to "developers and ops working together," but
    the CGOA exam expects the more precise formulation from The DevOps
    Handbook: DevOps is organized around "Three Ways" — three flows of work
    and information that GitOps directly strengthens.

         [The DevOps Three Ways, mapped onto mechanisms you already know]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ THE THREE WAYS                          HOW GITOPS IMPLEMENTS IT             │
         │ 1. Flow (work moves left to right,      Pull-based deployment (Ch1.5)        │
         │    Dev → Ops → Production, with         removes the manual handoff/          │
         │    minimal friction and handoffs)       ticket step between Dev and Ops      │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ 2. Feedback (amplify signals moving     The Feedback Loop (Ch31.6) —         │
         │    right to left, Production back       notifications, health status,        │
         │    to Dev, as fast as possible)         and observability, all built in      │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ 3. Continuous Learning &                Git's immutable commit history       │
         │    Experimentation (build a culture     (Ch2) gives every incident a         │
         │    of continual improvement)            full audit trail to learn from       │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    This mapping is why CGOA classifies GitOps as a DevOps practice rather
    than a competing methodology: it doesn't replace DevOps culture, it
    gives all Three Ways a concrete technical implementation inside the
    deployment stage specifically.

    32.4  DevSecOps
    DevSecOps extends the Three Ways with a fourth thread woven through all
    of them: security is not a final, separate gate before production — it's
    built into every stage of Flow, Feedback, and Continuous Learning from
    the start.

    ┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ 📖 KEY TERM: DevSecOps                                                  │
    │ The practice of integrating security practices throughout the software │
    │ delivery lifecycle — rather than as a single late-stage review — so    │
    │ that every change is verified for security as it moves, not after it   │
    │ arrives.                                                               │
    └────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    This guide has already taught three concrete DevSecOps mechanisms, just
    without the umbrella label:

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ 1. PR review as a security gate (Ch2)                                        │
         │    Every change to the State Store passes through a pull request —           │
         │    a human (or required approvers via CODEOWNERS) reviews it BEFORE          │
         │    it can ever reach the cluster. Security review happens pre-merge,         │
         │    not post-deploy.                                                          │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ 2. Signed commits & supply-chain verification (Ch2, Ch24)                    │
         │    GPG/SSH-signed commits prove WHO authored a change and that it            │
         │    wasn't tampered with in transit — critical because, in GitOps,            │
         │    Git IS the deployment trigger, so Git's integrity IS production's         │
         │    integrity (Ch24's 4-layer security model).                                │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ 3. Policy as Code via OPA/Gatekeeper (Ch27)                                  │
         │    Admission-time policy enforcement acts as a backstop that CANNOT          │
         │    be skipped, even if a reviewer misses something at PR time —              │
         │    security expressed as versioned, testable code, not a checklist.          │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    32.5  CI and CD, Precisely Defined
    Chapter 31.1 distinguished the ADJECTIVE "continuous" in four senses.
    This section defines CI and CD as PRACTICES — process ownership and
    tooling boundaries — which is how the exam's Related Practices domain
    actually tests them.

         [CI and CD as practices — GitOps is specifically the CD half]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ CONTINUOUS INTEGRATION (CI)             CONTINUOUS DELIVERY/DEPLOYMENT (CD)  │
         │ Practice: merge + build + test          Practice: get a validated change     │
         │   frequently, catch breakage early        into production reliably           │
         │ Owned by: the app repo, developers      Owned by: the config repo, GitOps    │
         │ Tooling: GitHub Actions/GitLab CI       Tooling: ArgoCD/FluxCD (GitOps IS    │
         │   build the image, run tests              the CD execution mechanism)        │
         │ Output: a tested container image,      Output: that image running live,      │
         │   pushed to a registry                    reconciled against Git forever     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    The single most exam-relevant sentence in this chapter: GitOps IS NOT
    CI, and GitOps IS NOT the only possible CD mechanism — it is ONE
    specific, pull-based way of implementing the CD practice. Chapter 25
    already showed the full pipeline handoff (CI builds and commits a new
    tag; GitOps takes it from there) — this section just gives each half of
    that handoff its precise, examinable name.

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD — Why CGOA Groups These 4 Practices Together
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    CaC, IaC, DevOps/DevSecOps, and CI/CD aren't a random grab-bag — the
    official curriculum groups them as "Related Practices" because each one
    answers the question "what does GitOps assume already exists, or sit
    next to?" GitOps assumes something has already provisioned the cluster
    (IaC's job), assumes a CI pipeline already produced a tested artifact
    (CI's job), and operates as a specific, opinionated implementation
    choice within the broader DevOps/DevSecOps culture — not a replacement
    for any of them. Exam questions in this domain are really testing
    whether you can draw the boundary correctly, not whether you can perform
    each practice.

    ⚠️ GOTCHAS
    ──────────
    • Calling any versioned YAML "GitOps" — that's CaC (32.1). It's only
      GitOps once an agent pulls it automatically and reconciles it
      continuously; CaC is necessary but not sufficient.
    • Assuming GitOps replaces your CI pipeline — it replaces the
      DEPLOYMENT step only (32.5). Build, test, and image-push still
      happen exactly as before, in GitHub Actions/GitLab CI/Jenkins.
    • Treating DevSecOps as a fourth pipeline stage bolted onto the end —
      the whole point (32.4) is that PR review, signed commits, and
      policy-as-code are woven through EVERY stage, not appended after CD.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
    ─────────────
    • If a question asks about provisioning a VM, cluster, or managed
      database, that's IaC (32.2) — if it asks about a Deployment,
      ConfigMap, or app setting, that's CaC/GitOps (32.1). Read for WHAT
      is being changed, not just whether YAML is involved.
    • "Three Ways" questions (32.3) map cleanly: Flow = pull removes
      handoffs, Feedback = the Feedback Loop, Learning = Git history as an
      audit trail. Memorize the mapping, not just the definitions.
    • DevSecOps questions (32.4) that mention "admission control" or
      "cannot be bypassed" point to policy-as-code (Ch27's OPA/Gatekeeper)
      specifically, not PR review, which CAN in theory be bypassed by a
      misconfigured branch protection rule.

    🔑 COMMAND REFERENCE
    ────────────────────
    None yet — this chapter is conceptual. Your first real
    commands appear in the next hands-on chapter.

    🏋️ PRACTICE DRILL — Classify the Practice
    ───────────────────────────────────────
    A CGOA-style question set names 5 scenarios and asks which single
    practice (CaC, IaC, DevOps, DevSecOps, or CI/CD) each one best
    illustrates — the same skill this domain tests on the real exam.
    TASK:
       Classify each: (1) a Terraform module provisioning a new VPC and
       EKS cluster from scratch; (2) an OPA/Gatekeeper policy blocking any
       Pod spec without resource limits at admission time; (3) a GitHub
       Actions workflow that runs unit tests on every pull request; (4) an
       engineering org restructuring its incident postmortems to
       explicitly capture and share lessons learned; (5) a Kustomize
       overlay changing an environment variable for the staging
       environment, applied only through ArgoCD's automated sync.
       (Answers: 1-IaC, 2-DevSecOps, 3-CI, 4-DevOps/Continuous Learning,
       5-CaC via GitOps.)

    📊 VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ ▸ CaC                                                                │
    │   Config as versioned text, applied by any means. GitOps = CaC       │
    │   + pull + continuous reconciliation.                                │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ IaC                                                                │
    │   Provisions the platform itself (Ch23/Crossplane) — a               │
    │   different layer than what CaC/GitOps configures.                   │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ DevOps Three Ways                                                  │
    │   Flow (pull removes handoffs), Feedback (the Feedback Loop),        │
    │   Continuous Learning (Git history as audit trail).                  │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ DevSecOps                                                          │
    │   Security woven through every stage: PR review (Ch2), signed        │
    │   commits (Ch24), policy-as-code (Ch27) — not a final gate.          │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ CI vs CD                                                           │
    │   CI builds/tests in the app repo. CD deploys via the config         │
    │   repo. GitOps is specifically the CD half.                          │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    Chapter 32 — CGOA Exam Focus:
         🎯 CaC vs GitOps boundary (GitOps = CaC + pull + continuous)
         🎯 IaC vs CaC layer distinction
         🎯 DevOps Three Ways mapped to concrete GitOps mechanisms
         🎯 DevSecOps's 3 mechanisms already covered: Ch2, Ch24, Ch27
         🎯 Precise CI vs CD boundary — GitOps is the CD half only


Chapter 33: Architecture & Tooling Patterns Beyond ArgoCD/Flux
--------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    🎯 Exam-Critical · ⏱️ ~2 hours · 🎯 CGOA: GitOps Patterns (20%) + Tooling (14%) · 🏢 Daily-Work: Evaluating tools/architectures beyond your default stack

    WHY THIS CHAPTER MATTERS
    ────────────────────────
    Chapters 1-30 taught ArgoCD and FluxCD in real depth — but the CGOA
    exam's Patterns and Tooling domains, together 34% of the exam, are
    deliberately tool-agnostic. They test whether you understand the
    PATTERNS these two tools implement well enough to recognize the same
    patterns in a tool you've never used. This chapter names those patterns
    explicitly and surveys the wider tooling landscape Ch1.6 only listed by
    name.

    🎯 LEARNING OBJECTIVES — by the end of this chapter you can:
    ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
      1. Contrast pull-based and event-driven reconciliation, and explain
         why event-driven is still 'pull' in GitOps terms
      2. Contrast in-cluster and external reconciler architecture,
         including ArgoCD's hub-and-spoke hybrid
      3. Name 2 real State Store alternatives to Git and when each is
         chosen
      4. Explain kapp-controller, Rancher Fleet, and Jenkins X well enough
         to place them on an exam scenario
      5. Distinguish Helm and Kustomize as packaging philosophies, not
         just as commands
      6. Identify the 3 official Interoperability categories:
         Notifications, Observability, CI

    📍 YOU ARE HERE
    ────────────────
    Part 9: The Complete CGOA Curriculum ▸ Chapter 3 of 3 ▸ Next: Appendix H (Curriculum Cross-Reference)

    33.1  Pull vs. Event-Driven Reconciliation
    Every reconciler in this guide has run on a timer — ArgoCD and Flux both
    poll the State Store every few minutes by default. The official
    curriculum names a second pattern this guide has already shown you in
    Chapter 11, without naming it as an architectural choice: event-driven
    reconciliation.

         [Pull-based vs event-driven — Ch11's webhook feature, reframed as a pattern]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ PULL-BASED (polling)                    EVENT-DRIVEN (webhook-triggered)     │
         │ Agent wakes up on a fixed interval      An external event (a Git push,       │
         │   (e.g. every 3 minutes) and checks       a webhook call) tells the          │
         │   the State Store for changes             agent to check RIGHT NOW           │
         │ Latency: up to one full interval        Latency: near-instant reaction       │
         │ Simple, needs no inbound network        Needs an inbound webhook             │
         │   exposure to the State Store              receiver (Ch11's ArgoCD API)      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    This is the single most commonly misunderstood point in the whole
    Patterns domain: event-driven reconciliation is NOT push-based CD. The
    webhook doesn't carry the new configuration to the cluster — it only
    tells the agent "something changed, go pull now instead of waiting." The
    agent still does the actual fetch-and-apply itself. GitOps's "Pulled
    Automatically" principle (Ch4) survives fully intact under event-driven
    reconciliation; only the TRIGGER TIMING changes, not who initiates the
    fetch. In production, most real deployments run BOTH — a webhook for
    fast reaction, and polling as a fallback in case the webhook delivery
    ever fails.

    33.2  In-Cluster vs. External Reconciler Architecture
    ArgoCD and FluxCD both run as Pods inside the cluster they manage, using
    a ServiceAccount for direct Kubernetes API access — this is the
    in-cluster reconciler pattern, and it's the only one Ch1-30 showed you
    directly. It is not the only valid architecture.

         [In-cluster vs external reconciler — an architectural axis, not a tool choice]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ IN-CLUSTER RECONCILER                  EXTERNAL RECONCILER                   │
         │ Runs as a Pod inside the managed        Runs OUTSIDE the managed             │
         │   cluster (ArgoCD/Flux, Ch1-20)          cluster entirely                    │
         │ Direct K8s API access via a             Reaches the cluster via an           │
         │   local ServiceAccount                   outbound-only agent connection      │
         │ You operate it (upgrades, HA,           A hub cluster or SaaS vendor         │
         │   resource limits are your job)          operates it for you                 │
         │ Best for: a single cluster, or a        Best for: fleets of many remote      │
         │   small number you directly manage        clusters, SaaS-managed GitOps      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    ArgoCD's hub-and-spoke multi-cluster mode (Ch14) is the exam's favorite
    trick question here: the ArgoCD control plane itself is IN-CLUSTER
    relative to the hub cluster it's installed on, but it acts as an
    EXTERNAL reconciler relative to every spoke cluster it manages remotely
    via each spoke's registered API credentials — one real ArgoCD deployment
    is simultaneously both patterns, depending on which cluster you're
    asking about. Fully external, SaaS-hosted GitOps control planes take
    this further: the vendor runs the reconciler entirely off-cluster, and
    your cluster runs only a thin, outbound-only agent — trading operational
    overhead for reduced control over the reconciler itself.

    33.3  State Store Alternatives to Git
    Chapter 31.5 named Git as the default State Store. Chapter 16.2 already
    listed FluxCD's OCIRepository and Bucket source kinds alongside
    GitRepository, without explaining why a team would ever choose them.
    Here is that explanation.

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ OCI REGISTRY (e.g. FluxCD's OCIRepository)                                   │
         │   What: manifests packaged and pushed as an OCI artifact into the            │
         │   same container registry that already stores your app images.               │
         │   Why choose it: one artifact system instead of two — reuse existing         │
         │   registry auth, scanning, and retention policy; content-addressable         │
         │   immutable digests are a natural fit for OCI, same as image tags.           │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ OBJECT STORAGE BUCKET (e.g. FluxCD's Bucket — S3/GCS/Azure Blob)             │
         │   What: a tarball of rendered manifests dropped into a bucket by an          │
         │   upstream pipeline stage, read directly by the reconciler.                  │
         │   Why choose it: fits pipelines where manifests are GENERATED, not           │
         │   hand-edited (no PR review needed for that stage); simpler IAM-based        │
         │   auth than Git access; usable in air-gapped/regulated environments          │
         │   where hosting a Git server isn't an option.                                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    The tradeoff in both cases: you lose Git's built-in PR review workflow
    and rich commit-by-commit diffing (Ch2) as the State Store's own audit
    trail. Teams choosing an alternative State Store for the RAW manifests
    typically still keep a Git repo further upstream — for the source
    templates or the pipeline config that PRODUCES the OCI artifact or
    bucket object — so review still happens, just one stage earlier than the
    reconciler's direct input.

    33.4  Reconciliation Engines Beyond ArgoCD and Flux
    Chapter 1.6's tool-landscape table named Jenkins X, Rancher Fleet, and
    Carvel kapp in one line each. The Tooling domain (14% of the exam)
    expects enough depth to place each one on a scenario, not just recognize
    the name.

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ ENGINE                  ARCHITECTURE               BEST FIT                  │
         │ kapp-controller         CRD-driven (App CR),       VMware Tanzu/Carvel       │
         │   (Carvel project)      3-way merge + ownership     ecosystem; layered       │
         │                         tracking; pairs with ytt     app packaging with      │
         │                         (templating) + kbld          ytt/kbld toolchain      │
         │                         (image resolution)                                   │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ Rancher Fleet           Lightweight per-cluster     Massive multi-           │
         │                         agent, GitRepo CR fans      cluster/edge fleets      │
         │                         out to thousands of          (thousands of           │
         │                         clusters from one source      remote clusters)       │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ Jenkins X               Opinionated, integrated     Teams wanting CI +       │
         │                         CI+CD+GitOps; automated     CD + GitOps as one       │
         │                         PR-based promotion between   pre-wired product,      │
         │                         Git-repo-per-environment      not stitched tools     │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    One honest note for real-world judgment (not just exam recall): Jenkins
    X's standalone adoption has declined as ArgoCD/Flux matured into more
    complete solutions on their own — it remains historically important as
    an early example of CI, CD, and GitOps shipped as one integrated
    product, which is exactly why the exam still references "alternatives"
    as a named Tooling subtopic: recognizing the PATTERN an alternative tool
    represents matters more than its current market share.

    33.5  Manifest Formats and Packaging Beyond YAML
    Every manifest in Ch1-30 was YAML. The Tooling domain's "Manifest Format
    and Packaging" subtopic expects you to know YAML is a choice, not a
    requirement — and to revisit Helm vs. Kustomize through a
    packaging-philosophy lens, not just a command-syntax one.

         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ Jsonnet — a data-templating language (JSON superset: variables,              │
         │   functions, imports) that COMPILES to JSON/YAML manifests. Reduces          │
         │   copy-pasted duplication across environments. Used by tools like            │
         │   Grafana Tanka.                                                             │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ CUE — a configuration language built for defining, generating, AND           │
         │   validating data, with type-safety built into the language itself           │
         │   (stronger built-in schema validation than Jsonnet). Growing                │
         │   adoption — e.g. the Timoni project builds a Helm-alternative               │
         │   packaging model on top of CUE.                                             │
         │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
         │ Plain JSON — valid input to kubectl apply directly. Sometimes                │
         │   preferred for MACHINE-GENERATED manifests because JSON round-trips         │
         │   predictably, avoiding YAML footguns like the "Norway problem"              │
         │   (an unquoted `no` silently parsing as boolean false).                      │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    Helm and Kustomize (already used hands-on in Ch8, Ch17, and throughout
    this guide) represent two competing PACKAGING philosophies, not just two
    CLIs: Helm templates raw text into YAML using its own templating engine,
    then distributes the result as a versioned "chart" — a packaging format
    conceptually similar to an OCI image for applications. Kustomize instead
    patches already-valid YAML through structural overlays, with no
    templating engine and no text-that-isn't-YAML-yet stage — avoiding an
    entire class of templating-syntax bugs Helm charts can hit, at the cost
    of less expressive parameterization for deeply conditional configs.

    33.6  Interoperability
    The Tooling domain's final named subtopic isn't a new mechanism — it's
    an official label for something Ch11, Ch19, Ch25, and Ch26 already
    built, one chapter at a time.

         [The 3 official Interoperability categories, matched to chapters you've already read]
         ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
         │ OFFICIAL INTEROPERABILITY CATEGORY      ALREADY COVERED IN                   │
         │ Notifications                           Ch11 (ArgoCD notifications),         │
         │                                          Ch19 (Flux Alerts)                  │
         │ Observability                           Ch26 (Prometheus/Grafana             │
         │                                          metrics + dashboards)               │
         │ Continuous Integration                  Ch25 (GitHub Actions/GitLab          │
         │                                          CI pipeline handoff)                │
         └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    The exam-relevant reframing: a GitOps tool's value isn't just its own
    reconciliation engine — it's also how cleanly it plugs into the
    ecosystem AROUND that engine. A question naming a specific 3rd-party
    system (PagerDuty, Datadog, a specific CI vendor) is testing whether you
    can place it into one of exactly these three official categories, not
    asking you to know that vendor's API.

    🔧 UNDER THE HOOD — Why the Exam Tests Patterns You've Never Run
    ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    It would be easy to assume the Patterns (20%) and Tooling (14%) domains
    just want more tool trivia — Fleet's YAML shape, kapp-controller's CRD
    fields. They don't. CGOA is vendor-neutral by design: the Linux
    Foundation wants certified engineers who can walk into a team using a
    tool they've never touched and still reason correctly about its
    architecture, because they recognize the PATTERN underneath — pull vs.
    event-driven, in-cluster vs. external, Git vs. an alternative State
    Store. Every tool in this chapter is a costume; the patterns in
    33.1-33.3 are what's actually being tested.

    ⚠️ GOTCHAS
    ──────────
    • Assuming event-driven reconciliation means the trigger PUSHES the
      new config into the cluster — it doesn't (33.1). It only tells the
      agent to pull sooner; the fetch-and-apply is still agent-initiated.
    • Assuming ArgoCD/Flux are always purely in-cluster — hub-and-spoke
      multi-cluster mode (33.2, Ch14) makes the SAME control plane an
      external reconciler relative to every spoke cluster it manages.
    • Forgetting that choosing an alternative State Store (33.3) doesn't
      eliminate the need for review — it usually just moves PR review one
      stage upstream, to whatever produces the OCI artifact or bucket
      object.

    ⚡ SPEED TIPS
    ─────────────
    • "Near-instant reaction to a Git push" in a question stem points to
      event-driven reconciliation (33.1) — but if the answer options
      include "the CI pipeline pushes the change directly," that's a trap;
      GitOps never abandons pull, even when event-driven.
    • "Thousands of remote clusters" or "edge fleet" in a scenario points
      to Rancher Fleet specifically (33.4) — it's the engine built for
      that scale, distinct from ArgoCD/Flux's typical target.
    • If a question contrasts "templating engine" against "structural
      overlay, no templating," that's Helm vs. Kustomize as a packaging
      philosophy (33.5) — answer from the philosophy, not just which
      command you'd personally run.

    🔑 COMMAND REFERENCE
    ────────────────────
    None yet — this chapter is conceptual. Your first real
    commands appear in the next hands-on chapter.

    🏋️ PRACTICE DRILL — Pattern Recognition on an Unfamiliar Tool
    ───────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
    A CGOA scenario describes a fictional tool called "Meridian": it runs as
    a managed cloud service (not inside your cluster), receives a webhook
    the instant a linked Git repo changes, connects to your cluster only via
    an outbound agent you install, and reads its manifests from an OCI
    registry rather than Git directly.
    TASK:
       Using only the patterns from this chapter (not any real knowledge
       of a tool by this name), classify Meridian on each of 3 axes: (1)
       pull-based or event-driven reconciliation trigger; (2) in-cluster
       or external reconciler architecture; (3) Git or an alternative
       State Store. (Answers: 1-event-driven, since the webhook triggers
       immediate action rather than waiting on a poll interval;
       2-external, since the reconciler itself runs in Meridian's cloud,
       not your cluster, reaching in only via an outbound agent; 3-an
       alternative State Store, specifically an OCI registry, per 33.3.)

    📊 VISUAL SUMMARY
    ────────────────
    ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
    │ ▸ Pull vs Event-Driven                                               │
    │   Event-driven only changes trigger TIMING — the agent still         │
    │   initiates the actual fetch. Never confused with push.              │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ In-Cluster vs External                                             │
    │   Hub-and-spoke ArgoCD is BOTH at once, depending which cluster      │
    │   you're asking about (Ch14).                                        │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ State Store Alternatives                                           │
    │   OCI registries and object buckets trade Git's PR review for        │
    │   registry/IAM reuse — review often just moves upstream.             │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ Alternative Engines                                                │
    │   kapp-controller (Carvel/Tanzu), Rancher Fleet (massive             │
    │   fleets), Jenkins X (integrated CI+CD+GitOps).                      │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ Packaging Philosophy                                               │
    │   Helm templates text into YAML then packages charts. Kustomize      │
    │   patches already-valid YAML — no templating stage.                  │
    │ ──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────── │
    │ ▸ Interoperability                                                   │
    │   3 official categories only: Notifications (Ch11/19),               │
    │   Observability (Ch26), CI (Ch25).                                   │
    └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

    Chapter 33 — CGOA Exam Focus:
         🎯 Pull vs. event-driven reconciliation — event-driven is still pull
         🎯 In-cluster vs. external reconciler, incl. the hub-and-spoke hybrid
         🎯 State Store alternatives to Git: OCI registries, object buckets
         🎯 Reconciliation engines beyond ArgoCD/Flux and their best-fit scale
         🎯 The 3 official Interoperability categories: Notifications, Observability, CI
